d\left(-\frac{1}{\omega}\theta\right)\left(\tilde{X},.\right)=-\frac{1}{\omega}\Omega\left(\tilde{X},.\right)=-\frac{1}{\omega}d\boldsymbol{\omega}\underset{(\ref{eq:def_Sigma_omega})}{=}0
.

2.4
Metric
g
on
T^{*}M

Let us suppose that we have an atlas
M=\bigcup_{j=1}^{J}U_{j}
where

U_{j}
are open subset on
M
such that for
j=1,\ldots J
,

\kappa_{j}:m\in U_{j}\rightarrow y=\left(x,z\right)\in V_{j}\subset\mathbb{R}_{x}^{\mathrm{dim}M-1}\times\mathbb{R}_{z}

(2.22)

are flow box coordinate, i.e.
\left(d\kappa_{j}\right)\left(X\right)=\frac{\partial}{\partial z}
.
On each chart
U_{j}
, let us denote
\eta=\left(\xi,\omega\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{dim}M-1}\times\mathbb{R}

the dual coordinates associated to
\left(x,z\right)
. Hence
\left(y,\eta\right)=\left(x,z,\xi,\omega\right)

are coordinates on
T^{*}\left(\mathbb{R}_{x}^{\mathrm{dim}M-1}\times\mathbb{R}_{z}\right)
.

Let us recall the definition and properties of the metric
g
on

T^{*}M
introduced in
[
20
, section 4.1.2]

with parameters that we take here
\alpha^{\perp}=\frac{1}{2}
and

\alpha^{\parallel}=0
. For
s\in\mathbb{R}
, we set

\langle s\rangle:=\left(1+s^{2}\right)^{1/2}\underset{\left|s\right|\gg 1}{\sim}\left|s\right|.

(2.23)

For
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{dim}M}
, let

\delta^{\perp}\left(\eta\right):=\left\langle\left|\eta\right|\right\rangle^{-1/2}.

(2.24)

In coordinates, we define the metric
g
on
T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{dim}M}=\mathbb{R}_{\varrho}^{2\mathrm{dim}M}

at a point
\varrho=\left(y,\eta\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{dim}M}\times\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{dim}M}

by

g\left(\varrho\right):=\left(\frac{dx}{\delta^{\perp}\left(\eta\right)}\right)^{2}+\left(\frac{d\xi}{\delta^{\perp}\left(\eta\right)^{-1}}\right)^{2}+dz^{2}+d\omega^{2}.

(2.25)

This gives a metric
g_{j}:=\left(d\kappa_{j}\right)^{*}g
on
T^{*}U_{j}
.
It is shown in
[
20
, Lemma 4.6]

that on each intersection
U_{j}\cap U_{j^{\prime}}
, the metric
g_{j}

and
g_{j^{\prime}}
are relatively bounded by each other uniformly in
\eta
.
Each metric
g_{j}
is compatible with the canonical symplectic form

\Omega
on
T^{*}M
given in (
2.13
).

Remark 2.7
.

An important property of the metric

g
that will be essential in our analysis is that the size of a
unit ball projects on the coordinates
x
as a set of size of order

\delta^{\perp}\left(\eta\right)=\left\langle\left|\eta\right|\right\rangle^{-1/2}

that goes to zero as
\left|\eta\right|\rightarrow+\infty
. See
[
20
, Figure 2.3]
.

We recall the definition that
\Omega,g
are compatible in Definition

C.13
.

Lemma 2.8
.

There exists a global smooth metric
g

on
T^{*}M
that is also compatible with
\Omega
and relatively
bounded with respect to every metric
g_{j}
on any chart
T^{*}U_{j}
.

Démonstration
.

Let
G
be a global metric on
T^{*}M
defined by

G:=\sum_{j=1}^{J}\left|\mathrm{det}d\kappa_{j}\right|\left(\chi_{j}^{2}\circ\kappa_{j}\circ\pi\right)\cdot g_{j}

(2.26)

where
\pi:T^{*}M\to M
denotes the bundle projection and
\chi_{j}\in C_{0}^{\infty}\left(\kappa_{j}\left(U_{j}\right);\mathbb{R}^{+}\right)

form a quadratic partition of unity on
M
, i.e.
\sum_{j}\chi_{j}^{2}\circ\kappa_{j}\left|\mathrm{det}d\kappa_{j}\right|=\boldsymbol{1}
,
see
[
20
, Lemma 4.2]
.
Recall that on each intersection
U_{j}\cap U_{j^{\prime}}
, the metric
g_{j}

and
g_{j^{\prime}}
are uniformly equivalent (i.e. relatively bounded) that
we write
g_{j}\asymp g_{j^{\prime}}
. Consequently, from finitness of the
sum (
2.26
), on any chart
T^{*}U_{j}
, we have

G\asymp g_{j}.

(2.27)

We define

K:=\check{\Omega}^{-1}\check{G}\quad:TT^{*}M\rightarrow TT^{*}M,

(2.28)

where
\check{G},\check{\Omega}:TT^{*}M\rightarrow T^{*}T^{*}M
are
defined from
G,\Omega
as in (
A.1
). We consider
the
polar decomposition

of
K
written
K=J\left|K\right|
where
\left|K\right|=\sqrt{K^{\dagger_{g}}K}

is positive definite Hermitian (bundle maps over
T^{*}M
) and
J

is an
almost complex structure

because, using that
\check{G}^{*}=\check{G}
and
\check{\Omega}^{*}=-\check{\Omega}

we get
K^{\dagger_{g}}\underset{(\ref{eq:adjoint})}{=}\check{G}^{-1}K^{*}\check{G}\underset{(\ref{eq:def_K-1})}{=}\check{G}^{-1}\check{G}^{*}\check{\Omega}^{*-1}\check{G}=-\check{\Omega}^{-1}\check{G}=-K
,
hence
J^{2}=\frac{K^{2}}{\left(K^{\dagger_{g}}K\right)}=-\mathrm{Id}
.
Since
g_{j}
is compatible with
\Omega
then
\check{g}_{j}=\check{\Omega}J_{j}

with
J_{j}^{2}=-\mathrm{Id}
. Thus
\check{\Omega}K\underset{(\ref{eq:def_K-1})}{=}\check{G}\underset{(\ref{eq:G_equiv})}{\asymp}g_{j}=\check{\Omega}J_{j}

gives
K\asymp J_{j}
then

\left|K\right|\asymp\mathrm{Id}.

(2.29)

We define the metric
g
by

\check{g}:=\check{\Omega}J\quad:TT^{*}M\rightarrow T^{*}T^{*}M.

(2.30)

By construction, the global metric
g
is smooth, compatible with

\Omega
and relatively bounded with respect to every
g_{j}
on
any chart
T^{*}U_{j}
because
\check{g}=\check{\Omega}J\underset{(\ref{eq:K_equiv})}{\asymp}\check{\Omega}J\left|K\right|=\check{\Omega}K\underset{(\ref{eq:def_K-1})}{=}\check{G}\asymp\check{g}_{j}
.
∎

We will denote
\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\rho\right)
the distance
between two points
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in T^{*}M
according to the metric

g
. It is shown in
[
20
, Lemma 4.5]

that the metric
g
is
geodesically complete
.

We will also use a property of slow variation of the metric
g
given
in
[
20
, Lemma 4.12]

and recalled later in (
2.59
). Finally
we have from
[
20
, Lemma 4.6]

the “Lipschitz property of
g
”, namely, for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
,
there exists a constant
C_{t}>0
such that for any
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in T^{*}M
,

\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\tilde{\phi}^{t}(\rho),\tilde{\phi}^{t}(\rho^{\prime})\right)\leq C_{t}\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho,\rho^{\prime}\right).

(2.31)

where
\tilde{\phi}^{t}:T^{*}M\rightarrow T^{*}M
is defined in (
2.8
).

2.5
Wave-packet transform
\mathcal{T}

As in
[
20
, def. 4.23]
,
for every coordinate chart
U_{j}\subset M
with
j\in\left\{1,\ldots,J\right\}

and
\rho\in T^{*}U_{j}
we define a wave packet
10
10

10

We refer to
[
20
, def. 4.23]

for the precise expression of a wave packet
\Phi_{j,\rho}
. Here
it is enough to say that on a chart
U_{j}
, for a given
\varrho=\left(y,\eta\right)\in T^{*}\mathbb{R}^{n+1}

and in local coordinates
y^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
, then for large

\left|\eta\right|\gg 1
, the function
\Phi_{j,\rho}
is equivalent
to a
Gaussian wave packet
in “vertical Gauge”:

\Phi_{j,\varrho}\left(y^{\prime}\right)\sim a_{\varrho}\chi\left(y^{\prime}-y\right)\exp\left(i\eta.\left(y^{\prime}-y\right)-\left\|y^{\prime}-y\right\|_{g_{\varrho}}^{2}\right)

(2.32)

with
y^{\prime}=\left(x^{\prime},z^{\prime}\right)\in\mathbb{R}^{n+1}
and where
\chi\in C_{0}^{\infty}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right)

is some cut-off function with
\chi\equiv 1
near the origin and
a_{\varrho}>0

is such that
\left\|\Phi_{j,\varrho}\right\|_{L^{2}\left(\mathbb{R}^{n+1}\right)}=1

and
\left\|y^{\prime}-y\right\|_{g_{\varrho}}^{2}=\left|z^{\prime}-z\right|^{2}+\left|\frac{\left(x^{\prime}-x\right)}{\delta^{\perp}\left(\eta\right)}\right|^{2}

is obtained from the metric (
2.25
).

\Phi_{j,\rho}\in C^{\infty}\left(M;\mathbb{C}\right)
and the
“wave
packet transform”

\mathcal{T}:\begin{cases}C^{\infty}\left(M;\mathbb{C}\right)&\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(T^{*}M;\mathbb{C}^{J}\right)\\
u&\rightarrow\left(\langle\Phi_{j,\rho}|u\rangle_{L^{2}\left(M\right)}\right)_{\rho\in T^{*}M,j\in\left\{1\ldots J\right\}}\end{cases},

(2.33)

that satisfies the ”
resolution of identity on
C^{\infty}\left(M\right)
”

[
20
, prop. 4.24]

\displaystyle\mathrm{Id}_{/C^{\infty}\left(M\right)}

\displaystyle=\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\mathcal{T}

(2.34)

where

\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}:L^{2}\left(T^{*}M\otimes\mathbb{C}^{J};\frac{d\rho}{\left(2\pi\right)^{\mathrm{dim}M}}\right)\rightarrow L^{2}\left(M;dm\right)

is the
L^{2}
-adjoint of
\mathcal{T}
where
d\rho\equiv dyd\eta

is the canonical measure on
T^{*}M
and
dm
an arbitray smooth
measure on
M
. We will introduce the
wave packet projector

onto the image of
\mathcal{T}
, see
[
20
, prop. 4.27]
:

\mathcal{P}:=\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}.

(2.35)

Remark 2.9
.

Below for simplicity, we will ignore
\otimes\mathbb{C}^{J}
in the
notation, i.e. ignore chart components, though keeping it in mind.
The operator
\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}:\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(T^{*}M\right)\rightarrow C^{\infty}\left(M\right)

is smoothing.

2.6
Description of the operator
e^{tX}
with the bundle
TT^{*}M

Remark 2.10
.

From now on we will often use the knowledge about Bargmann transform
that is summarized in Section
C
of
the appendix and we ask the readers to check the notation and their
knowledge. We will often refer to this Section
C
.

We first introduce a definition that will be used to express that
some operator
R
is “under control” or “negligible” in our
analysis. It will mean that the Schwartz kernel of
\mathcal{T}R\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}

decays very fast outside the graph of
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
defined
in (
2.8
) and moreover that on the graph, the Schwartz
kernel is bounded by the polynomial growth
\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{m}
.

Definition 2.11
.

Let
t\in\mathbb{R}
and
m\in\mathbb{R}
. We
define
\Psi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}}^{m}
as the set of operators
R:C^{\infty}\left(M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(M\right)

such that for any
N>0
, there exists a constant
C_{N,t}>0
such
that for any
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in T^{*}M

\displaystyle\left|\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\mathcal{T}R\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\delta_{\rho}\rangle_{L^{2}\left(T^{*}M\right)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{N,t}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{m}.

(2.36)

A useful consequence of (
2.36
) is the following
Lemma that uses the truncation operator
\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\omega}\right)

defined in (
1.25
).

Lemma 2.12
.

If
R\in\Psi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}}^{m}

with some
m<0
, then
\exists C_{t}>0
,

\left\|R\left(\mathrm{Id}-\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\omega}\right)\right)\right\|_{L^{2}\left(M\right)}\leq C_{t}\omega^{m}\underset{\omega\rightarrow+\infty}{\rightarrow}0.

(2.37)

Démonstration
.

We follow the same notations and techniques as in
[
20
, section 4.2]
.
We have that

\displaystyle\left\|R\left(\mathrm{Id}-\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\omega}\right)\right)\right\|_{L^{2}\left(M\right)}

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:resol_ident_Pi_rho})}{=}\left\|\mathcal{T}R\left(\mathrm{Id}-\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\omega}\right)\right)\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\right\|_{L^{2}\left(T^{*}M\right)}

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:def_P_wave_packet_projector})}{=}\left\|\mathcal{T}R\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\left(\mathcal{P}-\mathcal{P}\chi_{\omega}\mathcal{P}\right)\right\|_{L^{2}\left(T^{*}M\right)}.

From
[
20
, Prop. 4.27]
,
we have
\forall N\geq 0,\exists C_{N}>0,\forall\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in T^{*}M
,

\left|\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\mathcal{P}\delta_{\rho}\rangle_{L^{2}\left(T^{*}M\right)}\right|\leq C_{N}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\rho\right)\right\rangle^{-N},

(2.38)

i.e. the Schwartz kernel of
\mathcal{P}

decays fast outside
the diagonal and is uniformly bounded on the diagonal. Using definition
of
\chi_{\omega}
in (
1.24
) we have (the exponent

1/2
comes from the metric
g
in (
2.25
)),

\forall N\geq 0,\exists C_{N}>0,\forall\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in T^{*}M
,

\left|\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\mathcal{P}\chi_{\omega}\mathcal{P}\delta_{\rho}\rangle_{L^{2}\left(T^{*}M\right)}\right|\leq C_{N}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\rho\right)\right\rangle^{-N}\left\langle\mathrm{max}\left(0,\omega^{-1/2}\left(\omega-\left\|\rho\right\|_{g_{M}}\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}.

Together with (
2.36
) we deduce that
\forall N\geq 0,\exists C_{N,t}>0,\forall\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in T^{*}M
,

\displaystyle\left|\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\mathcal{T}R\left(\mathrm{Id}-\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\omega}\right)\right)\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\delta_{\rho}\rangle_{L^{2}\left(T^{*}M\right)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{N,t}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{m}

\displaystyle\qquad\left\langle\mathrm{max}\left(0,\omega^{-1/2}\left(\omega-\left\|\rho\right\|_{g_{M}}\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}.

Using Shur Lemma
[
20
, Lemma 4.38]
,
that estimates the
L^{2}
norm operator from the Schwartz kernel,
we deduce that if
m<0
, then
\exists C_{t}>0
,

\left\|R\left(\mathrm{Id}-\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\omega}\right)\right)\right\|_{L^{2}\left(M\right)}\leq C_{t}\omega^{m}\underset{\omega\rightarrow+\infty}{\rightarrow}0.

∎

2.6.1
Propagation of singularities. First estimate for the wave front set.

The next theorem is similar to the description of evolution of the

wave-front set

in micro-local analysis as in
[
43
, Prop. 9.5, page 29.]

and often called “
propagation of singularities
”.
It gives some first description of the pull back operator
e^{tX}

that we will need to improve later. Recall the notation
\Psi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}}^{0}

from definition
2.11
.

Theorem 2.13

(
Propagation of singularities
)
.

[
20
, Thm 4.51]
.
For each
t\in\mathbb{R}
,

e^{tX}\in\Psi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}}^{0}.

(2.39)

Remark 2.14
.

If
\rho^{\prime}
and
\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)
are in bounded
distance from each other, Theorem
2.13

says nothing very informative for the value of the Schwartz kernel

\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\mathcal{T}e^{tX}\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\delta_{\rho}\rangle_{L^{2}\left(T^{*}M\right)}
.
The next Theorem
2.16
below will
complete this lack of information by giving an approximate expression
for the Schwartz kernel of
\mathcal{T}e^{tX}\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}

in the neighborhood of the graph of
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
, and this
will be useful later to get Theorem
3.15
.
We first need to introduce some operators.

2.6.2
Definition of some operators

Below we view the (usual)
space of Schwartz functions

\mathcal{S}\left(TT^{*}M\right)
as the set of functions
u:\rho\in T^{*}M\rightarrow u_{\rho}\in\mathcal{S}\left(T_{\rho}T^{*}M\right)
,
i.e. we have a natural identification, with induced topology,

\mathcal{S}\left(TT^{*}M\right)\equiv\mathcal{S}\left(\rho\in T^{*}M;\mathcal{S}\left(T_{\rho}T^{*}M\right)\right).

(2.40)

The operator
\widetilde{\exp^{\circ}}
.

The Riemannian manifold
\left(T^{*}M,g\right)
is geodesically complete

[
20
, Lemma 4.5]
. Let
us write

\exp:TT^{*}M\rightarrow T^{*}M

(2.41)

for the
exponential map

associated to the metric
g
on
T^{*}M
of Lemma
2.8
.
From this map we have the pull back operator
\exp^{\circ}:\mathcal{S}\left(T^{*}M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(TT^{*}M\right)

but we consider instead the
twisted pull back operator
defined
as follows

\widetilde{\exp^{\circ}}:=e^{i\varphi}\exp^{\circ}\quad:C^{\infty}\left(T^{*}M\right)\rightarrow C^{\infty}\left(TT^{*}M\right),

(2.42)

with a phase function
\varphi:TT^{*}M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
that
corresponds to a change of trivialization to pass from the vertical
gauge given by the wave-packet transform (
2.32
)
to the local radial gauge as explained in section
C.2
.
Explicitly, this phase function
\varphi
is given by, for
\rho\in T^{*}M
,

v\in T_{\rho}T^{*}M
,

\varphi\left(v\right):=-\theta\left(v\right)-\frac{1}{2}D^{2}f\left(v\right)

(2.43)

with the Liouville one form
\theta\left(v\right)=\rho\left(d\pi\left(v\right)\right)

given in (
2.11
) and in the second part the function

f:=\theta\circ\left(d\exp\right)_{\rho}-\theta_{\rho}:T_{\rho}T^{*}M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}

made with the differential
\left(d\exp\right)_{\rho}:T_{\rho}T^{*}M\rightarrow TT^{*}M

(with the canonical identification of vector spaces
TT_{\rho}T^{*}M\equiv T_{\rho}T^{*}M
).
One has
f\left(0\right)=0
and
Df\left(0\right)=0
, so the quadratic
form
D^{2}f:T_{\rho}T^{*}M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}
called
Hessian
,
is well defined.

Remark 2.15
.

Geometrically the phase
\varphi
reflects the existence of a (trivial)
complex line bundle
L
over
T^{*}M
with connection given by the
canonical Liouville one form with respect to a global trivialization.
This line bundle is usually called the prequantum line bundle. As
mentioned before in Remark
1.1
, we can
ignore this line bundle since it is trivial so we can use a global
section, giving phases like in (
2.42
).To
understand better the phase
\varphi
in (
2.42
)
it may be useful to express it in local coordinates. Let
y\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{dim}M}

be local coordinates on
M
as in (
2.22
) and
\eta\in\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{dim}M}

be dual coordinates on
T^{*}M
. For a (local) flat metric
g
on

T^{*}M
one has
\rho^{\prime}=\exp_{\rho}\left(v\right)=\left(y_{\rho}+y_{v},\eta_{\rho}+\eta_{v}\right)

hence
v^{\prime}=\left(d\exp\right)_{\rho}\left(v\right)=\left(y_{v},\eta_{v}\right)
.
The Liouville one-form is
\theta_{\rho}\left(v^{\prime}\right)=\eta_{\rho}y_{v}

and
\theta_{\rho^{\prime}}\left(v^{\prime}\right)=\eta_{\rho^{\prime}}y_{v}=\left(\eta_{\rho}+\eta_{v}\right)y_{v}

giving the function
f\left(v\right)=\eta_{v}y_{v}
which is quadratic,
i.e.
D^{2}f=f
(due to absence of non linearities in this simple
setting of flat metric). The first term in (
2.43
)
is
e^{-i\theta_{\rho}\left(v\right)}=e^{-i\eta_{\rho}y_{v}}
and
is there to remove high oscillations coming from wave-packet transform
at point
\rho\in T^{*}M
. The second term in (
2.43
)
is
e^{-\frac{i}{2}D^{2}f\left(v\right)}=e^{-\frac{i}{2}\eta_{v}y_{v}}

and is used to pass from vertical gauge to radial gauge in accordance
with formula (
C.6
). In summary, for
Euclidean metric the phase function is

\varphi_{\rho}\left(y_{v},\eta_{v}\right)=-\eta_{\rho}y_{v}-\frac{1}{2}\eta_{v}y_{v}.

(2.44)

Truncation operator
\chi^{\lambda}
.

Let
0<\lambda<1/2
that is fixed
11
11

11

From the result (
2.49
) and estimate (
2.37
),
we see that
\lambda
close to zero gives better results, but in
this paper it is enough to take
\lambda=1/4
.
in this paper. The operator
\chi^{\lambda}
truncates functions
in a ball of radius
12
12

12

Here and after,
\left|\rho\right|=\left\|\rho\right\|_{g_{M}}

is measured with respect to an arbitrary metric
g_{M}
on
M
.

\left\langle\left\|\rho\right\|_{g_{M}}\right\rangle^{\lambda/2}

centered on the zero section of
TT^{*}M
:

\chi^{\lambda}:=\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\{\left\|.\right\|_{g_{\rho}}\leq\left\langle\left\|\rho\right\|_{g_{M}}\right\rangle^{\lambda/2}\right\}}:\mathcal{S}\left(TT^{*}M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(TT^{*}M\right),

(2.45)

i.e. for
u\in\mathcal{S}\left(TT^{*}M\right)
,
\rho\in T^{*}M
,

v\in T_{\rho}T^{*}M
,

\left(\chi^{\lambda}u\right)\left(v\right)=\begin{cases}u\left(v\right)&\text{ if }\left\|v\right\|_{g_{\rho}}\leq\left\langle\left\|\rho\right\|_{g_{M}}\right\rangle^{\lambda/2}\\
0&\text{ otherwise.}\end{cases}.

(2.46)

The restriction operator
r_{0}
.

Let

r_{0}:\mathcal{S}\left(TT^{*}M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(T^{*}M\right)

be the map that restricts a function
u\in\mathcal{S}\left(TT^{*}M\right)

(written
u\left(\rho,v\right)
,
\rho\in T^{*}M
,
v\in T_{\rho}T^{*}M
),
to its value at the zero section
\left(r_{0}u\right)\left(\rho\right):=u\left(\rho,0\right)
.

The metaplectic operator
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)
.

Recall the map
\tilde{\phi}^{t}:T^{*}M\rightarrow T^{*}M
defined
in (
2.8
). Its differential
d\tilde{\phi}^{t}:TT^{*}M\rightarrow TT^{*}M

gives a push-forward operator that we denote
\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)^{-\circ}:\mathcal{S}\left(TT^{*}M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(TT^{*}M\right)
.
As in (
C.17
) we define the “metaplectic correction”

\Upsilon\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right):=\left(\mathrm{det}\left(\frac{1}{2}\left(\mathrm{Id}+\left(\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)^{-1}\right)^{\dagger}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)^{-1}\right)\right)\right)^{1/2},

that is a positive function on
T^{*}M
. We also denote
\mathcal{P}:\mathcal{S}\left(TT^{*}M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(TT^{*}M\right)

the Bergman projector in radial gauge as defined in (
C.26
),
that is here a fiber-wise operator. As in Definition
C.36
,
we define the fiber-wise metaplectic operator over the map
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
:

\displaystyle\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:def_op_tilde})}{=}\left(\Upsilon\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)\right)^{1/2}\,\mathcal{P}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)^{-\circ}\mathcal{P}.

(2.47)

2.6.3
Linear approximation of the kernel

In the next theorem we use and compose the operators defined in the
previous section
2.6.2
with the operator

\mathcal{T}
in (
2.33
), and obtain a good approximation
of the pull back operator
e^{tX}
that improves the information
given in Theorem
2.13
. The rough idea
of this approximation is based on two properties: first, for
\rho\in T^{*}M
,
with
\left\|\rho\right\|_{g_{M}}\gg 1
, the metric is slowly
varying hence the metric
g
can be approximated by a Euclidean metric
in a neighborhood of
\rho
. Second, the unit balls of the metric

g
, transversely to the flow, have very small size projected on
the base
M
(see Remark
2.7
), hence
the dynamics can be approximated by its linearization i.e. by the
action of the differential
d\tilde{\phi}^{t}
.

Theorem 2.16

(
“More precise expression for the propagation of singularities”
)
.

For any
0<\lambda<\frac{1}{2}
,
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

e^{tX}=\left(\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}r_{0}\right)\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)\left(\chi^{\lambda}\widetilde{\exp^{\circ}}\mathcal{T}\right)+R_{t}

(2.48)

with some operator
R_{t}
that satisfies

R_{t}\in\Psi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}}^{-\frac{1}{2}+\lambda}.

(2.49)

Remark 2.17
.

Since
-\frac{1}{2}+\lambda<0
, we can apply Lemma
2.12

that guaranties that the remainder operator
R_{t}
restricted to
high frequencies is arbitrary small in operator norm.

Remark 2.18
.

The proof below shows that we also have the
simpler expression

e^{tX}=\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\left(\Upsilon\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)\right)^{1/2}\left(\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)^{-\circ}\mathcal{T}+R_{t}

(2.50)

with the push-forward operator
\left(\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)^{-\circ}:\mathcal{S}\left(T^{*}M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(T^{*}M\right)

and the multiplication operator by the metaplectic correction
\left(\Upsilon\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)\right)^{1/2}:\mathcal{S}\left(T^{*}M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(T^{*}M\right)
.
We already have commented this expression in (
1.11
).
One reason for us to later use (
2.48
) instead of (
2.50
)
is that (
2.48
) does not contains
\left(\Upsilon\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)\right)^{1/2}
,
but contains instead the operator
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)

that is fiberwise unitary in
L^{2}
, see appendix
C.7

(in fact
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)
contains

\left(\Upsilon\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)\right)^{1/2}
). On
the other hand, one advantage of (
2.50
) compare to (
2.48
)
is that it deals directly with function in
\mathcal{S}\left(T^{*}M\right)

instead of the more elaborate space
\mathcal{S}\left(TT^{*}M\right)
.
Later we will meet a similar situation, see Remark
3.20
.

Démonstration
.

Let
0<\lambda<\frac{1}{2}
and
t\in\mathbb{R}
. We consider the
operator
R_{t}
defined in (
2.48
), lifted to the cotangent
space:

\displaystyle\tilde{R}_{t}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{T}R_{t}\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:etX})}{=}\mathcal{T}e^{tX}\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}-\left(\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\right)r_{0}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)\left(\chi^{\lambda}\widetilde{\exp^{\circ}}\right)\left(\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\right).

(2.51)

According to Definition
2.11
, to get (
2.49
),
we have to show the following estimate for the Schwartz kernel of

\tilde{R}_{t}
, that for any
N>0
, there exists a constant
C_{N,t}>0

such that for any
\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in T^{*}M

\displaystyle\left|\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\tilde{R}_{t}\delta_{\rho}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq C_{N,t}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}+\lambda}.

(2.52)

We will split the computation in two parts: (A) far from the graph
of
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
and (B) near the graph.

Step (A), far from the graph of
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
.

Let us show that
\forall\lambda^{\prime}>\lambda
,
\exists C_{t,\lambda^{\prime}}
,

\forall\rho,\rho^{\prime}\in T^{*}M
,

\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)>C_{t,\lambda^{\prime}}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{\lambda^{\prime}/2}\Rightarrow\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|r_{0}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)\left(\chi^{\lambda}\widetilde{\exp^{\circ}}\right)\delta_{\rho}\rangle=0.

(2.53)

From definition of the exponential map we have
\forall\rho^{\prime\prime}\in T^{*}M,v^{\prime\prime}\in T_{\rho^{\prime\prime}}T^{*}M
,

\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime\prime},\text{exp}\left(\left(\rho^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\right)\right)\right)=\left\|v^{\prime\prime}\right\|_{g_{\rho^{\prime\prime}}}.

(2.54)

From definitions of
\chi^{\lambda}
and
\widetilde{\exp^{\circ}}
,
we have that
\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime\prime}}|\left(\chi^{\lambda}\widetilde{\exp^{\circ}}\right)\delta_{\rho}\rangle\neq 0

iff
\text{exp}\left(\left(\rho^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime\prime}\right)\right)=\rho
and

\text{{dist}}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime\prime},\rho\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:dist_g})}{=}\left\|v^{\prime\prime}\right\|_{g_{\rho^{\prime\prime}}}\underset{(\ref{eq:def_Chi_sigma-2})}{\leq}\left\langle\left|\rho^{\prime\prime}\right|\right\rangle^{\lambda/2}.

(2.55)

The operator
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)

is a bundle map over the map
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
hence
\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime},v^{\prime}}|\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)\delta_{\rho^{\prime\prime},v^{\prime\prime}}\rangle

vanishes if
\rho^{\prime}\neq\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho^{\prime\prime}\right)
. Hence,
setting
\rho^{\prime\prime}=\tilde{\phi}^{-t}\left(\rho^{\prime}\right)
, we have that

\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|r_{0}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)\left(\chi^{\lambda}\widetilde{\exp^{\circ}}\right)\delta_{\rho}\rangle\neq 0\quad\Rightarrow\quad\text{{dist}}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:invariance_g})}{\asymp}\text{{dist}}_{g}\left(\tilde{\phi}^{-t}\left(\rho^{\prime}\right),\rho\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:dist_})}{\leq}\left\langle\left|\rho^{\prime\prime}\right|\right\rangle^{\lambda/2}.

(2.56)

From
[
20
, Lemma 4.14]

we have that
\forall 0<\lambda<1
,
\forall\epsilon>0,\exists C_{\epsilon}>0
,

\forall\rho,\rho^{\prime}
,

\text{{dist}}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime\prime},\rho\right)\leq\left\langle\left|\rho^{\prime\prime}\right|\right\rangle^{\lambda/2}\Rightarrow\left\langle\left|\rho^{\prime\prime}\right|\right\rangle\leq C_{\epsilon}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{1+\epsilon},

hence the right hand side of (
2.56
) becomes
\text{{dist}}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\leq C_{t,\lambda^{\prime}}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{\lambda^{\prime}/2}

for any
\lambda^{\prime}>\lambda
and we get (
2.53
).

We assume that
\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)>C_{t,\lambda^{\prime}}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{\lambda^{\prime}/2}

i.e. points
\left(\rho,\rho^{\prime}\right)\in T^{*}M\times T^{*}M
are
“far” from the graph of
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
. We have that
e^{tX}\in\Psi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}}^{0}

and
\left(\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}r_{0}\right)\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)\left(\chi^{\lambda}\widetilde{\exp^{\circ}}\mathcal{T}\right)\in\Psi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}}^{0}
.
This implies that
R_{t}\in\Psi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}}^{0}
. For any

N>0
, take
M>\frac{1}{\lambda^{\prime}}
and
N^{\prime}=N+M
. We have

\displaystyle\left|\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\tilde{R}_{t}\delta_{\rho}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:Psi_tilde})}{\leq}C^{\prime}_{N^{\prime},t}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N^{\prime}}\leq C^{\prime\prime}_{N^{\prime},t}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-M}

\displaystyle\underset{(\mathrm{hyp.})}{\leq}C^{\prime\prime}_{N^{\prime},t}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}\left(C_{t,\lambda^{\prime}}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{\lambda^{\prime}/2}\right)^{-M}

\displaystyle\leq C^{\prime\prime\prime}_{N^{\prime},t,\lambda^{\prime}}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{-M\lambda^{\prime}/2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{N,t}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{-1/2}

that gives (
2.52
).

Step (B), near the graph of
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
.

We assume now that

\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\leq C_{t,\lambda^{\prime}}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{\lambda^{\prime}/2}

(2.57)

with
\lambda<\lambda^{\prime}<1
, i.e. points
\left(\rho,\rho^{\prime}\right)\in T^{*}M\times T^{*}M

are “near” the graph of
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
. Let us explain the
strategy that we will pursue. Using the slow variation of the metric

g
given in
[
20
, Lemma 4.12]
,
we will (1) approximate the metric
g_{\rho^{\prime}}
by the Euclidean metric
at point
\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)
. From (
2.25
),
the projected points
\pi\left(\rho^{\prime}\right),\pi\left(\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)

on
M
are at distance from each other that is
O\left(\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{\lambda^{\prime}/2-1/2}\right)

transversely to the flow direction and this distance goes to zero
if
\left|\rho\right|\rightarrow\infty
. So we will (2) approximate
the map
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
by its differential, i.e. neglect non-linear
terms. Finally (3), even for a linear map on an Euclidean space, we
need to show that the effect of the cutoff
\chi^{\lambda}
is negligible.

Having these three approximations in mind we write

\displaystyle\left|\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\tilde{R}_{t}\delta_{\rho}\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:Rtilde_t})}{\leq}R_{1}+R_{2}+R_{3},

with

R_{1}=\left|\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\left(\mathcal{T}e^{tX}\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}-\mathcal{B}_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}e^{tX}\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{\dagger}\right)\delta_{\rho}\rangle\right|

R_{2}=\left|\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\left(\mathcal{B}_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}e^{tX}\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{\dagger}-e^{-i\varphi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{\rho}^{t}\right)e^{i\varphi_{\rho}}\right)\delta_{\rho}\rangle\right|

(2.58)

R_{3}=\left|\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\left(e^{-i\varphi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{\rho}^{t}\right)e^{i\varphi_{\rho}}-\left(\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\right)r_{0}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)\left(\chi^{\lambda}\widetilde{\exp^{\circ}}\right)\left(\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\right)\right)\delta_{\rho}\rangle\right|,

where
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}=\mathcal{B}_{\left(V\right),\rho}:\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{\mathrm{dim}M}\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(\mathbb{R}^{2\mathrm{dim}M}\right)

is the Bargman transform in vertical gauge (
C.8
)
using local charts on
M
and defined from the Euclidean metric
g_{\rho}
.
We have the metaplectic operator
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{\rho}^{t}\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:def_op_tilde})}{=}\left(\Upsilon\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{\rho}^{t}\right)\right)^{1/2}\,\mathcal{P}_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{\rho}^{t}\right)^{-\circ}\mathcal{P}_{\rho}

with the Bergman projector in radial gauge
\mathcal{P}_{\rho}\underset{(\ref{eq:Bergman_projector})}{=}\mathcal{B}_{\left(R\right)\rho}\mathcal{B}_{\left(R\right),\rho}^{\dagger}
.
We have the phase function
\varphi_{\rho}
defined in (
2.43
)
and expressed in (
2.44
) for the Euclidean metric, that
is to pass from vertical gauge to radial gauge.

1.

In the term
R_{1}
, the operator
\mathcal{T}
is constructed from
the metric
g
whereas the operator
\mathcal{B}_{g}
is constructed
from the local Euclidean metric
g_{\rho}
in
\mathcal{B}_{\rho}
.
We use
[
20
, Lemma 4.12]

that the metric
g
varies slowly on
T^{*}M
: for any
0\leq\gamma<1
,
there exist
N>0
and
C>0
such that for any
\varrho,\varrho^{\prime}\in\mathbb{R}^{2\left(n+1\right)}

and
v\in\mathbb{R}^{2\left(n+1\right)}
,

\displaystyle\max\left\{\frac{\|v\|_{g_{\varrho^{\prime}}}}{\|v\|_{g_{\varrho}}},\frac{\|v\|_{g_{\varrho}}}{\|v\|_{g_{\varrho^{\prime}}}}\right\}\leq 1+C\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{-\left(1-\gamma\right)/2}\left\langle\left\langle\left|\varrho\right|\right\rangle^{-\gamma/2}\left\|\varrho^{\prime}-\varrho\right\|_{g_{\varrho}}\right\rangle^{N}.

(2.59)

If we take
\gamma=\lambda+\epsilon
with
\epsilon>0
, we get that
for
\left\|\varrho^{\prime}-\varrho\right\|_{g_{\varrho}}<\left\langle\left|\varrho\right|\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{2}\left(\lambda+\epsilon\right)}

then

\left|\frac{\|v\|_{g_{\varrho^{\prime}}}}{\|v\|_{g_{\varrho}}}-1\right|\leq C\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}\left(1-\lambda-\epsilon\right)},

hence
\left|R_{1}\right|\leq C_{N,t}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}+\lambda}
.

2.

For the term
R_{2}
, we start from

\mathcal{B}_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}e^{tX}\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{\dagger}\underset{(\ref{eq:def_exptX})}{=}\mathcal{B}_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}\left(\phi^{t}\right)^{\circ}\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{\dagger}

and in local chart in a small neighborhood of
\pi\left(\rho\right),\pi\left(\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\in M

of size

O_{t}\left(\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{\frac{\lambda^{\prime}}{2}}\delta^{\perp}\left(\rho\right)\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:def_delta})}{=}O_{t}\left(\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{-\left(1-\lambda^{\prime}\right)/2}\right),

(2.60)

we approximate
\phi^{t}
by its differential
d\phi_{\rho}^{t}

at
\rho
, replace
\left(\phi^{t}\right)^{\circ}
by
\left(d\phi^{t}\right)_{\rho}^{\circ}
.
In the next lines,
\left(V\right),\left(R\right)
denote respectively
vertical and radial gauge defined in section
C.2
.
In the first equality, we substract the main oscillatory term
e^{-i\theta_{\rho}\left(.\right)}
.
In the last equality we use that
\mathrm{det}\left(d\phi^{t}\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:volume_form_M})}{=}1
.
We write

\displaystyle\mathcal{B}_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}\left(d\phi^{t}\right)_{\rho}^{\circ}\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle=e^{i\theta_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}\left(.\right)}\mathcal{B}_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}^{\left(V\right)}\left(d\phi^{t}\right)_{\rho}^{\circ}\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{\dagger\left(V\right)}e^{-i\theta_{\rho}\left(.\right)}\underset{(\ref{eq:Gaussian_wave_packet-V-R},\ref{eq:def_Bargman_B},\ref{eq:phase_phi})}{=}e^{-i\varphi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}}\mathcal{B}_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}^{\left(R\right)}\left(d\phi^{t}\right)_{\rho}^{\circ}\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{\dagger\left(R\right)}e^{i\varphi_{\rho}}

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:Op_tilde_PHI})}{=}e^{-i\varphi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{\rho}^{t}\right)e^{i\varphi_{\rho}}

In the approximation replacing
\mathcal{B}_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}e^{tX}\mathcal{B}_{\rho}^{\dagger}

by
e^{-i\varphi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{\rho}^{t}\right)e^{i\varphi_{\rho}}
,
we have neglected the non linearity of the map
\phi^{t}
. From Taylor
expansion this gives that

\left|R_{2}\right|\underset{(\ref{eq:size})}{\leq}C_{N,t}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}\left(\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{-\left(1-\lambda^{\prime}\right)/2}\right)^{2},

smaller than previous terms.

3.

For the term
R_{3}
, one first operation is to pass to the tangent
bundle with
\widetilde{\exp^{\circ}}
and with a truncation
\chi^{\lambda}

and past restriction
r_{0}
. For this, in the linear setting, Lemma

C.40
shows that in case of a linear map
A:E\rightarrow E

and Euclidean metric
g
on
E\oplus E^{*}
, a cutoff
\chi_{\sigma}

gives an error term bounded by
C_{N}\sigma^{-N},\forall N
. Here

\sigma=\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{\lambda/2}
,
so this gives
O_{N,t}\left(\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{-N}\right)
,
smaller than previous terms. Another operation is to pass again to
the manifold with the metric
g
as in step 1. We have seen that
it gives
\left|R_{3}\right|\leq C_{N,t}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}+\lambda}
.

We have checked (
2.52
) that gives (
2.49
).
∎

3
Micro-local analysis of a contact
vector field
X
on
\left(M,\mathcal{A}\right)
near
\Sigma=\mathbb{R}\mathcal{A}\backslash\left\{0\right\}

In this section we pursue the analysis of a smooth non vanishing vector
field
X
on a closed manifold
M
done in section
2
)
and furthermore we assume that
X
is a
Reeb vector field
,
i.e. there is a smooth contact one form
\mathcal{A}
on
M
such
that for every
m\in M
, the linear space
\mathrm{Ker}\left(\mathcal{A}\left(m\right)\right)

endowed with the two form
\left(d\mathcal{A}\right)\left(m\right)

is a linear symplectic space and

\mathcal{A}\left(X\right)=1,\qquad d\mathcal{A}\left(X,.\right)=0.

(3.1)

This implies that
\mathcal{A}
is invariant under the flow
\phi^{t}

generated by X. Indeed the Lie derivative vanishes:
\mathcal{L}_{X}\mathcal{A}=\iota_{X}d\mathcal{A}+d\iota_{X}\mathcal{A}=d\mathcal{A}\left(X,.\right)+d1=0
.
Also
\mathrm{dim}M=2d+1
, with
2d=\mathrm{dim}\left(\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\right)
.
We will write

dm:=\frac{1}{d!}\mathcal{A}\wedge\left(d\mathcal{A}\right)^{\wedge d}

(3.2)

for the corresponding smooth and non-degenerate volume form on
M

invariant by the flow
\phi^{t}
.

Remark 3.1
.

For the moment we do not assume that
X
is Anosov, but later in
section
4
, this one form
\mathcal{A}

will be determined by
\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}=E_{u}\oplus E_{s}

in (
4.4
). A typical example of contact vector field

X
on
\left(M,\mathcal{A}\right)
is a general geodesic vector
field on the unit cotangent bundle
M=\left(T^{*}\mathcal{N}\right)_{1}

of a Riemannian manifold
\mathcal{N}
with
\mathcal{A}
being
the
Liouville one-form
.

In
T^{*}M
we define

\displaystyle E_{0}^{*}

\displaystyle:=\left(\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\right)^{\perp}=\left\{\rho\in T^{*}M,\quad\mathrm{Ker}\rho\supset\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\right\}

(3.3)

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:Ker_A})}{=}\mathbb{R}\mathcal{A}=\left\{\omega\mathcal{A}\left(m\right),\quad m\in M,\omega\in\mathbb{R}\right\}.

(3.4)

E_{0}^{*}
is a rank
1
sub-bundle of
T^{*}M
over
M
. We
have

T^{*}M=\mathrm{Ker}X\oplus E_{0}^{*},

(3.5)

where

\mathrm{Ker}X=\left\{\rho\in T^{*}M,\quad\boldsymbol{\omega}\left(\rho\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:omega_function})}{=}\rho\left(X\right)=X\left(\rho\right)=0\right\}

(3.6)

is a rank
2d
sub-bundle of
T^{*}M
over
M
.

3.1
The symplectization
\Sigma=\mathbb{R}\mathcal{A}\backslash\left\{0\right\}

We first recall the important following lemma. It shows that
\Sigma:=\mathbb{R}\mathcal{A}\backslash\left\{0\right\}=E_{0}^{*}\backslash\left\{0\right\}

is a symplectic sub-manifold of
T^{*}M
, called the
symplectization

of
M

[
7
, Section 11.2]
[
1
, Appendix 4.]
.
We will denote
\pi:T^{*}M\rightarrow M
the projection and
\pi^{\circ}\left(dm\right)

the form
dm
(
3.2
) pulled back on
T^{*}M
.

Lemma 3.2
.

The set

\Sigma:=\mathbb{R}\mathcal{A}\backslash\left\{0\right\}:=\left\{\omega\mathcal{A}\left(m\right)\in T^{*}M\,\mid\quad m\in M,\omega\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\left\{0\right\}\right\}

(3.7)

is a smooth
symplectic sub-manifold
of
T^{*}M
with
\mathrm{dim}\Sigma=2\left(d+1\right)
.
The induced volume form on
\Sigma
is given by

d\varrho:=\frac{1}{\left(d+1\right)!}\left(d\theta\right)^{\wedge\left(d+1\right)}=\omega^{d}\left(d\omega\right)\wedge\pi^{\circ}\left(dm\right)

(3.8)

where
\theta
is the Liouville form (
2.11
).

Démonstration
.

For
m\in M
,
\omega\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\left\{0\right\}
,
at point
\rho=\omega\mathcal{A}\left(m\right)\in\Sigma\subset T^{*}M

we have

\theta_{\rho}\underset{(\ref{eq:def_theta})}{=}\omega\left(\rho\right)\cdot\pi^{\circ}\mathcal{A}

(3.9)

where
\pi^{\circ}
is the pull back map on forms. Hence
d\theta=d\left(\omega\left(\pi^{\circ}\mathcal{A}\right)\right)=d\omega\wedge\left(\pi^{\circ}\mathcal{A}\right)+\omega\left(\pi^{\circ}d\mathcal{A}\right)

giving the following volume form on
E_{0}^{*}

\displaystyle d\varrho:

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\left(d+1\right)!}\left(d\theta\right)^{\wedge\left(d+1\right)}=\frac{1}{\left(d+1\right)!}\left(d+1\right)\omega^{d}\cdot d\omega\wedge\left(\left(\pi^{\circ}\mathcal{A}\right)\wedge\left(\pi^{\circ}d\mathcal{A}\right)^{\wedge d}\right)

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:volume_form_M})}{=}\omega^{d}\left(d\omega\right)\wedge\pi^{\circ}\left(dm\right)

which does not vanish on
\Sigma=E_{0}^{*}\backslash\left\{0\right\}

since for the line bundle
E_{0}^{*}\rightarrow M
,
dm
is a measure
on the base
M
and
d\omega
is a measure on the fibers. Consequently

d\theta
restricted to
\Sigma
is
non degenerate
,

\Sigma
is a smooth sub-manifold of
T^{*}M
.
∎

Since the one form
\mathcal{A}
is invariant under the flow
\phi^{t}
,
the set
\Sigma
is invariant under the lifted flow
\tilde{\phi}^{t}

in
T^{*}M
defined in (
2.8
). The purpose of this
section
3
is to provide a useful
approximation of the pullback operator
e^{tX}
restricted to a micro-local
neighborhood of
\Sigma
(i.e. vicinity of
\Sigma
in
T^{*}M
)
in terms of the differential map
d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}
that is

d\tilde{\phi}^{t}
restricted to the symplectic normal bundle
N

of
\Sigma
.

Remark 3.3
.

Later in section
4
we will consider
a contact Anosov flow and see in Lemma
4.2
that

\Sigma
is the trapped set or non wandering set for the lifted flow

\tilde{\phi}^{t}
. Consequently we will obtain in Theorem
4.6

that the dynamics
e^{tX}
outside
\Sigma
is negligible in some
sense, so the only important part is indeed a micro-local neighborhood
of
\Sigma
that we consider in this section.

3.2
The symplectic normal bundle
N\rightarrow\Sigma

3.2.1
Metaplectic decomposition
K\overset{\perp_{\Omega}}{\oplus}N

We have seen in Lemma
3.2
that
\Sigma:=E_{0}^{*}\backslash\left\{0\right\}

is symplectic. We will denote

T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M:=\left\{T_{\rho}\left(T^{*}M\right)\,\mid\quad\rho\in\Sigma\right\}

that is the tangent bundle
T\left(T^{*}M\right)
restricted to the
base space
\Sigma
. The following Lemma provides a decomposition
of
T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M
into symplectic sub-bundles, invariant under
the flow map
d\tilde{\phi}^{t}:T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\rightarrow T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M

with
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
defined in (
2.8
). For the
one form
\mathcal{A}
seen as a map
\mathcal{A}:M\rightarrow T^{*}M
,
we will use its differential map
d\mathcal{A}:TM\rightarrow TT^{*}M
.
Recall that
\tilde{X}
was defined in section
2.3.4
.

Lemma 3.4
.

Let
\tilde{E}_{0}:=\mathbb{R}\tilde{X}
and
\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}:=T\mathbb{R}\mathcal{A}

be rank 1 sub-bundles of
T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M
. Then

K_{0}:=\tilde{E}_{0}\oplus\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}

(3.10)

is a symplectic sub-bundle of
T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M
and we have a decomposition
of
T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M
as an orthogonal sum of symplectic spaces
K,K_{0},N
:

T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M=\underbrace{K\overset{\perp_{\Omega}}{\oplus}K_{0}}_{T\Sigma}\overset{\perp_{\Omega}}{\oplus}N

(3.11)

\mathrm{dim}K=2d,\quad\mathrm{dim}K_{0}=2,\quad\mathrm{dim}N=2d,

invariant under the flow map
d\tilde{\phi}^{t}:T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\rightarrow T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M
.

Remark 3.5
.

Notice that
N=\left(T\Sigma\right){}^{\perp_{\Omega}}
is the
\Omega
-symplectic
orthogonal to
T\Sigma=K\overset{\perp_{\Omega}}{\oplus}K_{0}
in

T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M
. To explain the notation of
\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}
,
notice that
K_{0}=\tilde{E}_{0}\oplus\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}
is
\Omega
-symplectic,
hence
\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}
is isomorphic to the dual of
\tilde{E}_{0}
.

Démonstration
.

For fixed
\omega\in\mathbb{R}\backslash\left\{0\right\}
, let

T\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\subset TT^{*}M
be the tangent space
to the graph of
\omega\mathcal{A}
:

T\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right):=\left(d\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\right)\left(TM\right).

For any
\rho=\omega\mathcal{A}\left(m\right)\in\Sigma
,
m=\pi\left(\rho\right)\in M
,
we have that
d\pi:T\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\left(\rho\right)\rightarrow TM\left(m\right)

is an isomorphism. We decompose

T\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)=K\oplus\tilde{E}_{0}

(3.12)

with
\tilde{E}_{0}=\mathbb{R}\tilde{X}=T\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\cap d\pi^{-1}\left(\mathbb{R}X\right),

\mathrm{dim}\tilde{E}_{0}=1,
and
K:=T\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\cap d\pi^{-1}\left(\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\right)
,

\mathrm{dim}K=2d
. We have
\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}=\mathbb{R}\mathcal{A}=T\Sigma\cap d\pi^{-1}\left(\left\{0\right\}\right)
,

\mathrm{dim}\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}=1
and

K_{0}=\tilde{E}_{0}\oplus\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}=T\Sigma\cap d\pi^{-1}\left(\mathbb{R}X\right).

(3.13)

We have
T\Sigma=T\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\oplus\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}=K\oplus K_{0}.

We have to prove that
K,K_{0}
are symplectic and that
K\perp_{\Omega}K_{0}
.
We first show the following Lemma, where for fixed
\omega
, the
one form
\omega\mathcal{A}
is seen as a map
\omega\mathcal{A}:M\rightarrow T^{*}M
,
with differential
d\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right):TM\rightarrow TT^{*}M
.

Lemma 3.6
.

At point
\omega\mathcal{A}\left(m\right)\in\Sigma
, the linear map

p:=\left(d\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\right)\circ\left(d\pi\right)\qquad:T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\rightarrow T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M

(3.14)

is a projector with

\mathrm{Ker}p=\mathrm{Ker}\left(d\pi\right),\quad\mathrm{Im}p=T\left(\mathrm{Im}\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\right).

(3.15)

We have

\left(d\pi\right)^{-1}\left(\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\right)=\left(T\mathbb{R}\mathcal{A}\right)^{\perp_{\Omega}}\underset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\left(\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}\right)^{\perp_{\Omega}}.

(3.16)

Démonstration
.

We have
\pi\circ\mathcal{A}=\mathrm{Id}_{M}
hence
\left(d\pi\right)\circ\left(d\mathcal{A}\right)=\mathrm{Id}_{TM}

and

p^{2}=\left(d\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\right)\underbrace{\left(d\pi\right)\left(d\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\right)}_{\mathrm{Id}_{TM}}\left(d\pi\right)=p.

We have

\Omega_{/\mathrm{Ker}\left(d\pi\right)}\underset{(\ref{eq:def_Omega-1})}{=}0.

(3.17)

For any
U\in T_{\omega\mathcal{A}}T^{*}M
,

\displaystyle\omega\mathcal{A}\left(\left(d\pi\right)\left(U\right)\right)

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:alpha_theta})}{=}\theta\left(\left(d\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\right)\circ\left(d\pi\right)\left(U\right)\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:def_Euler})}{=}\Omega\left(\mathcal{E},\left(d\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\right)\circ\left(d\pi\right)\left(U\right)\right)

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:def_projector_p})}{=}\Omega\left(\mathcal{E},p\left(U\right)\right)=\Omega\left(\mathcal{E},\left(p\left(U\right)-U\right)+U\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:Ker_p_Ker_dpi},\ref{eq:Omega_ker_dpi})}{=}\Omega\left(\mathcal{E},U\right).

On
\Sigma=\mathbb{R}\mathcal{A}
we have
\mathbb{R}\mathcal{E}=\mathbb{R}\mathcal{A}
,
we deduce (
3.16
).
∎

We have an isomorphism

d\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right):TM=\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\oplus\mathbb{R}X\rightarrow T\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:TomegaA})}{=}K\oplus\tilde{E}_{0},

and

\left(d\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\right)^{\circ}\Omega\underset{(\ref{eq:dalpha_Omega})}{=}d\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right).

(3.18)

Hence

d\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right):\left(\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A},\omega d\mathcal{A}\right)\rightarrow\left(K,\Omega\right)

is a symplectomorphism for the respective symplectic structures. Since

\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}
is
d\mathcal{A}-
symplectic, this implies
that
K
is
\Omega-
symplectic. This also gives that
\Omega\left(K,\tilde{E}_{0}\right)=\omega d\mathcal{A}\left(\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A},\mathbb{R}X\right)=0
.
By definition we have
\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}=\mathbb{R}\mathcal{A}
and

K=T\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\cap d\pi^{-1}\left(\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\right)
.
From (
3.16
) we have that
\Omega\left(K,\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}\right)=0
.
Since
K_{0}=\tilde{E}_{0}\oplus\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}
, we have obtained
that
K\perp_{\Omega}K_{0}
and that
K_{0}
is
\Omega-
symplectic.
This complete the proof of Lemma
3.4
.
∎

The next Lemma concerns specifically the subspace
K\overset{\perp_{\Omega}}{\oplus}N

in (
3.11
). We show that this space is also the
sum of canonical Lagrangian spaces
H\oplus V
(horizontal and vertical).
This is illustrated on figure
3.1
. Recall the frequency
function
\boldsymbol{\omega}:\rho\in T^{*}M\rightarrow\boldsymbol{\omega}\left(\rho\right)=\rho\left(X\right)\in\mathbb{R}

defined in (
2.7
) and that
\left(\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A},d\mathcal{A}\right)

is a linear symplectic space from section
4.1.2
.

Definition 3.7
.

For
u\in\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}
, we define the maps

\displaystyle\overline{K}\left(u\right)

\displaystyle:=\left(\left(d\pi^{-1}\right)\left(u\right)\right)\cap K,\quad\overline{N}\left(u\right):=\left(\left(d\pi^{-1}\right)\left(u\right)\right)\cap N

\displaystyle\overline{H}\left(u\right)

\displaystyle:=\frac{1}{2}\left(\overline{K}\left(u\right)+\overline{N}\left(u\right)\right),\quad\overline{V}\left(u\right):=\frac{1}{2}\left(\overline{K}\left(u\right)+\overline{N}\left(-u\right)\right),

(3.19)

that define the horizontal and the vertical spaces:

\displaystyle H

\displaystyle:=\left\{\overline{H}\left(u\right),u\in\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\right\},

\displaystyle V

\displaystyle:=\left\{\overline{V}\left(u\right),u\in\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\right\}

that are subspaces of
K\overset{\perp_{\Omega}}{\oplus}N
.

Lemma 3.8

(Metaplectic decomposition)
.

At any point
\omega\mathcal{A}\in\Sigma
with
\omega\neq 0
,
let

\widetilde{\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}:=\left(d\pi^{-1}\left(\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\right)\right)\cap\mathrm{Ker}\left(d\boldsymbol{\omega}\right).

(3.20)

\widetilde{\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}
is a
\Omega-
symplectic subspace
of
T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M
and we have the decompositions

\widetilde{\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}=K\overset{\perp_{\Omega}}{\oplus}N=H\oplus V,

(3.21)

with
\Omega-
symplectic and orthogonal subspaces
K,N
and
\Omega-
Lagrangian
subspaces
H,V
with
\mathrm{dim}V=\mathrm{dim}H=2d
.

We have
\left(d\pi\right)\left(H\right)=\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}

and
\left(d\pi\right)\left(V\right)=\left\{0\right\}
. The linear
maps

\sqrt{\omega}d\pi:\left(K,\Omega\right)\rightarrow\left(\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A},d\mathcal{A}\right)

(3.22)

\sqrt{\omega}d\pi:\left(N,\Omega\right)\rightarrow\left(\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A},-d\mathcal{A}\right)

(3.23)

are symplectomorphism for the respective symplectic structures.

Figure
3.1:
Illustration of Lemma
3.8
about the
vector bundle
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}\subset T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\overset{\pi}{\rightarrow}M
.
We have the linear map
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}\overset{d\pi}{\rightarrow}\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\subset TM

and
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}
is a sum of two symplectic
subspaces
K\oplus N
and two Lagrangian subspaces
H\oplus V
.
This structure is preserved by the dynamics.

Démonstration
.

We have

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:Ker_tilde_A})}{=}\left(d\pi^{-1}\left(\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\right)\right)\cap\mathrm{Ker}\left(d\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:lemma_KerA})}{=}\left(\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}\right)^{\perp_{\Omega}}\cap\mathrm{Ker}\left(d\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:decomp_K_K0_N})}{=}\left(K\oplus\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}\oplus N\right)\cap\mathrm{Ker}\left(d\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)

(3.24)

We have
d\boldsymbol{\omega}\left(N\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:eq5})}{=}\Omega\left(\tilde{X},N\right)\underset{(\mathrm{def})}{=}\Omega\left(\tilde{E}_{0},N\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:decomp_K_K0_N})}{=}0
.
This gives
N\subset\mathrm{Ker}\left(d\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)

and similarly
K\subset\mathrm{Ker}\left(d\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)
.
We have
\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}=\mathbb{R}\mathcal{E}
and

d\boldsymbol{\omega}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:eq5})}{=}\Omega\left(\tilde{X},\mathcal{E}\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:def_Euler})}{=}\theta\left(\tilde{X}\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:omega})}{=}-\omega\neq 0,

hence
\tilde{E}_{0}^{*}\cap\mathrm{Ker}\left(d\boldsymbol{\omega}\right)=0
.
We deduce the decomposition
\widetilde{\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}=K\overset{\perp_{\Omega}}{\oplus}N

in (
3.21
).

On the space
K
we have

\displaystyle\Omega_{/K}

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:def_projector_p},\ref{eq:TomegaA})}{=}p^{\circ}\Omega_{/K}\underset{(\ref{eq:def_projector_p})}{=}\left(\left(d\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\right)\circ\left(d\pi\right)\right)^{\circ}\Omega_{/K}

\displaystyle=\left(d\pi\right)^{\circ}\left(d\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)\right)^{\circ}\Omega_{/K}\underset{(\ref{eq:dA_Omega})}{=}\left(d\pi\right)^{\circ}d\left(\omega\mathcal{A}\right)=\omega\left(d\pi\right)^{\circ}\left(d\mathcal{A}\right).

This gives (
3.22
) since
d\mathcal{A}
is bilinear. For

u\in\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}
, we have
\left(d\pi\right)\left(\overline{V}\left(u\right)\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:def_H_V})}{=}\frac{1}{2}\left(u-u\right)=0
,
hence
V
is vertical hence
\Omega-
Lagrangian. For the map
\overline{V}:\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\rightarrow V
,
we have
\overline{V}\circ d\pi_{V\rightarrow\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}=\mathrm{Id}_{/V}

and
V
is
\Omega-
Lagrangian hence

0=\Omega_{/V}=\left(d\pi_{V\rightarrow\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}\right)^{\circ}\circ\left(\overline{V}\right)^{\circ}\Omega\underset{(\ref{eq:def_H_V})}{=}\frac{1}{2}\left(d\pi_{V\rightarrow\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}\right)^{\circ}\circ\left(\left(\overline{K}\right)^{\circ}\left(\Omega\right)+\left(-\mathrm{Id}\right)^{\circ}\circ\left(\overline{N}\right)^{\circ}\left(\Omega\right)\right).

Since
\overline{K}=\left(d\pi\right)_{\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\rightarrow K}^{-1}

and
\left(-\mathrm{Id}\right)^{\circ}=\mathrm{Id}
on bilinear forms
we deduce

\left(\overline{N}\right)^{\circ}\left(\Omega\right)=-\left(\overline{K}\right)^{\circ}\left(\Omega\right)=-\left(d\pi\right)_{\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\rightarrow K}^{-1}\left(\Omega\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:dpi_K})}{=}-\omega d\mathcal{A},

giving (
3.23
) because
\overline{N}=\left(d\pi\right)_{\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\rightarrow N}^{-1}
.
For the map
\overline{H}:\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}\rightarrow H
,
we have
\overline{H}\circ d\pi_{H\rightarrow\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}=\mathrm{Id}_{/H}

hence

\displaystyle\Omega_{/H}

\displaystyle=\left(d\pi_{H\rightarrow\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}\right)^{\circ}\circ\left(\overline{H}\right)^{\circ}\Omega\underset{(\ref{eq:def_H_V})}{=}\frac{1}{2}\left(d\pi_{H\rightarrow\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}\right)^{\circ}\circ\left(\left(\overline{K}\right)^{\circ}\left(\Omega\right)+\left(\overline{N}\right)^{\circ}\left(\Omega\right)\right)

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:dpi_K},\ref{eq:dpi_N})}{=}\frac{1}{2}\left(d\pi_{H\rightarrow\mathrm{Ker}\mathcal{A}}\right)^{\circ}\left(\omega d\mathcal{A}-\omega d\mathcal{A}\right)=0.

Hence
H
is
\Omega-
Lagrangian.
∎

3.2.2
Modified metric
\tilde{g}
on the set
\Sigma

In (
3.11
), the decomposition
K\overset{\perp_{\Omega}}{\oplus}K_{0}\overset{\perp_{\Omega}}{\oplus}N

is orthogonal for the symplectic form
\Omega
. But a priori it is
not orthogonal for the metric
g
given in (
2.25
).
Since this last property would be useful later, we will modify the
metric
g
according to the next lemma to get
g-
orthogonality
as well.

Lemma 3.9
.

On the set
\Sigma
, we construct a
modified metric
\tilde{g}
from the existing metric
g
as follows.
Let
\tilde{g}_{/N}=g_{/N}
,
\tilde{g}_{/K}=g_{/K}
,
\tilde{g}_{/K_{0}}=g_{/K_{0}}

and assume
K\overset{\perp_{\tilde{g}}}{\oplus}K_{0}\overset{\perp_{\tilde{g}}}{\oplus}N
.
Then

1.

\tilde{g}
is compatible with
\Omega
.

2.

The decomposition (
3.11
) is orthogonal with respect
to the metric
\tilde{g}
(and w.r.t.
\Omega
as before):

T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M=K\overset{\perp_{\Omega,\tilde{g}}}{\oplus}K_{0}\overset{\perp_{\Omega,\tilde{g}}}{\oplus}N

(3.25)

3.

The modified metric
\tilde{g}
is equivalent to the metric
g
,
uniformly with respect to
\rho\in\Sigma
for
\left|\omega\right|\geq 1
.

Démonstration
.

By construction we have claim 1,2. In local coordinates
\left(x,z,\xi,\omega\right)

used in section
2.4
, and for some
\omega_{0}\gg 1
,
consider the change of coordinates
x^{\prime}=\omega_{0}^{1/2}x,z^{\prime}=z
,
\xi^{\prime}=\omega_{0}^{-1/2}\xi
,

\omega^{\prime}=\omega
so that
\Omega=dx^{\prime}\wedge d\xi^{\prime}+dz^{\prime}\wedge d\omega^{\prime}
.
Let
\rho=\omega\mathcal{A}\left(m\right)\in\Sigma
with
\left|\omega\right|\asymp\omega_{0}
.
From (
2.24
) we have
\delta^{\perp}\left(\rho\right)\asymp\left|\rho\right|^{-1/2}\asymp\left|\omega\right|^{-1/2}\asymp\omega_{0}^{-1/2}
.
Then
g\underset{(\ref{eq:metric_g_tilde_in_coordinates})}{\asymp}dx^{\prime 2}+d\xi^{\prime 2}+dz^{\prime 2}+d\omega^{\prime 2}
,
where the equivalence are uniform w.r.t.
\left|\omega\right|\geq 1
.
Using this new system of coordinates we get that the subspaces
K,K_{0},N

are uniformly apart form each over with respect to
\rho\in\Sigma
,
i.e. for the pair of spaces
\left(K,N\right)
, we have
\exists 0<C<1,

\forall\rho\in\Sigma
,
\forall u\in K_{\rho}
,
\forall v\in N_{\rho}
,

\left|g_{\rho}\left(u,v\right)\right|<C\left\|u\right\|_{g_{\rho}}\left\|v\right\|_{g_{\rho}},

(3.26)

and similarly for other pair of space
\left(K,K_{0}\right),\left(K_{0},N\right)
.
This gives Claim 3.
∎

For simplicity of notations, from now on,
we assume that the
metric
g
on
T^{*}M
has the properties 1,2,3
of Lemma
3.9

on
\Sigma
.

3.2.3
Factorization formula for
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)_{/\Sigma}\right)

We consider now the operator
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)

that appears on the right hand side of (
2.48
). Since
\Sigma

in invariant by
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
and
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)

is fiber-wise, we can consider its restriction to
\mathcal{S}\left(T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\right)
.
It is denoted and given by

\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)_{/\Sigma}\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:Op_tilde})}{=}\left(\Upsilon\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)\right)^{1/2}\,\mathcal{P}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)^{-\circ}\mathcal{P}\quad:\mathcal{S}\left(T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\right)

The decomposition (
3.11
) is orthogonal for both
the symplectic form and the modified metric
\tilde{g}
, and preserved
by the differential of the flow, i.e.

\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)_{/\Sigma}=\underbrace{d\tilde{\phi}^{t}{}_{K}+d\tilde{\phi}^{t}{}_{K_{0}}}_{d\tilde{\phi}_{T\Sigma}^{t}}+d\tilde{\phi}^{t}{}_{N}.

(3.27)

Remark 3.10
.

Eq. (
3.11
) implies that
on the set
\Sigma
we have a natural identification

\mathcal{S}\left(T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\right)=\mathcal{S}\left(K\right)\otimes_{\Sigma}\mathcal{S}\left(K_{0}\right)\otimes_{\Sigma}\mathcal{S}\left(N\right).

(3.28)

where the right hand side is a notation for the space of sections

\mathcal{S}\left(\Sigma;\mathcal{S}\left(K\right)\otimes\mathcal{S}\left(K_{0}\right)\otimes\mathcal{S}\left(N\right)\right)
,
i.e. the tensor product is fiber-wise over
\Sigma
and not global.

From invariant Lagrangian decomposition (
3.21
), (
3.10
)
and Lemma
C.22
we have

\mathcal{P}=\mathcal{P}_{K}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{K_{0}}\otimes\mathcal{P}_{N}.

Then (
C.36
) is used to define
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{K}^{t}\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:def_op_tilde})}{=}\left(\Upsilon\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{K}^{t}\right)\right)^{1/2}\,\mathcal{P}_{K}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{K}^{t}\right)^{-\circ}\mathcal{P}_{K}

and similarly
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{K_{0}}^{t}\right),\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right)
.

Theorem 3.11

(
Factorization formula
)
.

On the set
\Sigma
, with respect
to the identification (
3.28
) we have

\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)_{/\Sigma}\right)=\underbrace{\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{K}^{t}\right)\otimes_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{K_{0}}^{t}\right)}_{\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{T\Sigma}^{t}\right)}\otimes_{\Sigma}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right).

(3.29)

Démonstration
.

Use previous factorization and (
C.19
) to get (
3.29
).
∎

3.3
Equivalent family of operators

As we have said, we want to describe only the restriction of the operator

e^{tX}
to a small neighborhood of the set
\Sigma\subset T^{*}M
,
i.e. to consider Schwartz kernel of operators in this neighborhood
and not on the whole manifold
T^{*}M
. For this purpose, we introduce
here an equivalence relation between operators denoted
\approx

that we will often use later. The equivalence
A\approx B
will mean
roughly that the two operators
A,B
are approximately the same at
high frequency and near the set
\Sigma
.

Let
\sigma>0
that will be chosen large enough later. Define the
following characteristic function
\chi_{\Sigma,\sigma}:T^{*}M\rightarrow\mathbb{R}^{+}

as follows. We write
\rho=\rho_{*}+\rho_{0}\in T^{*}M
with transverse
component
\rho_{*}\in\mathrm{Ker}X
and frequency component
\rho_{0}\in E_{0}^{*}
,
see (
3.5
). We set

\displaystyle\chi_{\Sigma,\sigma}\left(\rho\right)

\displaystyle:=\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\{\left\|\rho_{*}\right\|_{g_{\rho}}\leq\sigma\right\}},

(3.30)

and

\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\Sigma,\sigma}\right):=\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\chi_{\Sigma,\sigma}\mathcal{T}\quad:C^{\infty}\left(M\right)\rightarrow C^{\infty}\left(M\right)

(3.31)

be the corresponding P.D.O. operator, as defined in
[
20
, def. 4.28]

that extracts components at distance less than
\sigma
from the
set
\Sigma
.

Recall
\Psi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}}^{m}
that has been defined in Definition

2.11
.

Definition 3.12
.

For two operators
A,B:C^{\infty}\left(M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(M\right)

we write

A\approx B

(3.32)

if there exists
m<0
,
t\in\mathbb{R}
, such that for any
\sigma>0
,

\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\Sigma,\sigma}\right)\left(A-B\right)\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\Sigma,\sigma}\right)\in\Psi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}}^{m}.

Remark 3.13
.

From
m<0
, we recall that consequently we have the estimate (
2.37
)
for
R=\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\Sigma,\sigma}\right)\left(A-B\right)\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\Sigma,\sigma}\right)
,
where the constant
C_{t}
depends on
\sigma
also.

3.4
Description of the operator
e^{tX}
near the set
\Sigma
with
the bundle
N

We first define some operators. The construction that we pursue below
is represented on Figure
1.1
. Using the metric
g

on
T^{*}M
, and considering the sub-bundle
N\subset T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M
,
we have the exponential map

\exp_{N}:N\rightarrow T^{*}M.

(3.33)

Due to the slow variation of the metric
g
at high frequencies,
given in (
2.59
), we have that for any

0\leq\mu<1
, and any frequency
\left|\omega\right|\geq\omega_{0}

with
\omega_{0}
large enough,
\exp_{N}
is a diffeomorphism on
the neighborhood
\left\|v\right\|_{g}\leq\left\langle\omega\right\rangle^{\mu/2}
.
To express this, let
0<\mu<1
and
\omega_{0}\geq 1
for this property.
We introduce the following cut-off function in frequency
\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}:N\rightarrow\left[0,1\right]

defined as follows. For
\rho\in\Sigma
,
v\in N_{\rho}
and
\omega=\boldsymbol{\omega}\left(\rho\right)
,

\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}\left(v\right)=\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\{\left\|v\right\|_{g}\leq\left\langle\omega\right\rangle^{\mu/2},\left|\omega\right|\geq\omega_{0}\right\}}.

(3.34)

The multiplication operator by
\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}
is also denoted

\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}:\mathcal{S}\left(N\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(N\right)
.
Later, in Theorem
4.37
, we will need
to take
\mu
small enough depending in particular on the Hölder
exponents of the Anosov dynamics.

Similarly to (
2.42
), we define the twisted
pull back and push forward operators

\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}\right)^{\circ}}:\mathcal{S}\left(T^{*}M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(N\right),

\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}^{-1}\right)^{\circ}}\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}:\mathcal{S}\left(N\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(T^{*}M\right).

Namely, for
u\in\mathcal{S}\left(T^{*}M\right)
,
w\in\mathcal{S}\left(N\right)
,
let
\rho\in T^{*}M
with
\rho=\exp_{N}\left(v_{2}\right)
,
v_{2}\in N_{\rho_{2}}
,

\rho_{2}\in\Sigma
,
\left|\boldsymbol{\omega}\left(\rho_{2}\right)\right|\geq\omega_{0}
,
and
\left\|v_{2}\right\|_{g}\leq\left\langle\boldsymbol{\omega}\left(\rho_{2}\right)\right\rangle^{\mu/2}
,

\left(\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}\right)^{\circ}}u\right)\left(v_{2}\right):=e^{i\varphi\left(v_{2}\right)}u\left(\rho\right),

(3.35)

\left(\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}^{-1}\right)^{\circ}}w\right)\left(\rho\right):=e^{-i\varphi\left(v_{2}\right)}w\left(v_{2}\right),

(3.36)

with phase function
\varphi
defined in (
2.43
),
to pass from vertical gauge to radial gauge. In the next definition,
we combine these previous operators together with
\mathcal{T}:C^{\infty}\left(M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(T^{*}M\right)

in (
2.33
).

Definition 3.14
.

We define the operators

\mathcal{T}_{N}:=\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}\right)^{\circ}}\mathcal{T}\quad:C^{\infty}\left(M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(N\right)

(3.37)

\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\Delta}:=\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}^{-1}\right)^{\circ}}\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}\quad:\mathcal{S}\left(N\right)\rightarrow C^{\infty}\left(M\right).

(3.38)

Using the operators (
3.37
),(
3.38
),
the next theorem gives a good approximation of the operator
e^{tX}

in a neighborhood of the set
\Sigma
in terms of the differential
map
d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}
on the normal bundle
N
. We will use
the notation
\approx
defined in (
3.32
) that compare
operators near
\Sigma
. We also use the shortened notation

\Upsilon_{t}:=\Upsilon\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{T\Sigma}^{t}\right),

(3.39)

that is the metaplectic correction (
C.17
) of the differential
restricted to
T\Sigma
,
d\tilde{\phi}_{T\Sigma}^{t}=d\tilde{\phi}^{t}{}_{K}+d\tilde{\phi}^{t}{}_{K_{0}}

that appears in (
3.27
). In the next theorem we view

\Upsilon_{t}^{1/2}>0
as a multiplication operator over
\Sigma
.

Theorem 3.15
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

e^{tX}\approx\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\Delta}\,\Upsilon_{t}^{1/2}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right)\mathcal{T}_{N}

(3.40)

Remark 3.16
.

Let us compare formula (
3.40
) with (
2.48
).
Both formula express the pull back operator
e^{tX}
from some quantum
(metaplectic) operator
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)

or
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right)
acting
on some vector bundle over
T^{*}M
. The difference is that (
2.48
)
is valid microlocally on the whole
T^{*}M
(but at high frequencies)
and general to any non vanishing vector fields
X
, whereas (
3.40
)
is specific to contact (or Reeb) vector field and valid microlocally
near
\Sigma\subset T^{*}M
(and high frequencies).

Remark 3.17
.

As a particular case, taking
t=0
in (
3.40
) gives
that

\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\Delta}\mathcal{T}_{N}\approx\mathrm{Id}.

(3.41)

Démonstration
.

As we already did in the proof of Theorem
2.16
,
we use
\mathcal{T}:C^{\infty}\left(M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(T^{*}M\right)

to lift the analysis to
T^{*}M
and consider the Schwartz kernel

\tilde{R}:=\left|\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\mathcal{T}\left(e^{tX}-\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\Delta}\Upsilon_{t}^{1/2}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right)\mathcal{T}_{N}\right)\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\delta_{\rho}\rangle\right|,

that we will estimate for points
\rho^{\prime},\rho\in T^{*}M
close to
the set
\Sigma
as needed by Definition
3.12
.
For this, let
\sigma>0
. We write
\rho=\rho_{*}+\rho_{0}
with
transverse component
\rho_{*}\in\mathrm{Ker}X
and frequency component

\rho_{0}\in E_{0}^{*}
, see (
3.5
), similarly
for
\rho^{\prime}
and we assume that

\left\|\rho_{*}\right\|_{g_{\rho}}\leq\sigma,\quad\left\|\rho^{\prime}_{*}\right\|_{g_{\rho}}\leq\sigma.

(3.42)

We also assume that points
\left(\rho,\rho^{\prime}\right)\in T^{*}M\times T^{*}M

are “near” the graph of
\tilde{\phi}^{t}
, i.e. that (
2.57
)
holds true. Otherwise the proof of Theorem
2.16
,
(step A), shows that far from the graph, the Schwartz kernel is totally
negligible.

As before, the strategy is to approximate the dynamical operator
e^{tX}

to its linear part, approximate the metric to an Euclidean metric,
and then check that formula holds true exactly in this linear and
Euclidean setting. For this, we use local coordinates in
\mathbb{R}^{2\left(2d+1\right)}

on
T^{*}M
and use the operator
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{\rho}^{t}\right)

and phase
\varphi_{\rho}
that have already been defined in (
2.58
).
We introduce intermediary operators

O_{1}:=\mathcal{T}e^{tX}\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\qquad O_{2}:=e^{-i\varphi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{\rho}^{t}\right)e^{i\varphi_{\rho}},

O_{3}:=e^{-i\varphi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}}\mathcal{P}_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}^{-1}\right)^{\circ}}\Upsilon_{t}^{1/2}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right)\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}\right)^{\circ}}\mathcal{P}_{\rho}e^{i\varphi_{\rho}},

\displaystyle O_{4}

\displaystyle:=\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}^{-1}\right)^{\circ}}\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}\Upsilon_{t}^{1/2}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right)\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}\right)^{\circ}}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}

\displaystyle\underset{(\ref{eq:def_T_N},\ref{eq:def_T_N_Delta})}{=}\mathcal{T}\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\Delta}\,\Upsilon_{t}^{1/2}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right)\mathcal{T}_{N}\mathcal{T}^{\dagger},

and their differences

R_{1}:=\left|\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\left(O_{1}-O_{2}\right)\delta_{\rho}\rangle\right|,\qquad R_{2}:=\left|\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\left(O_{2}-O_{3}\right)\delta_{\rho}\rangle\right|,

(3.43)

R_{3}:=\left|\langle\delta_{\rho^{\prime}}|\left(O_{3}-O_{4}\right)\delta_{\rho}\rangle\right|.

So we have

\tilde{R}\leq R_{1}+R_{2}+R_{3}.

For the term
R_{1}
, in the proof of Theorem
2.16
,
(step B), we have already shown that for any
0<\lambda<\frac{1}{2}
,

R_{1}\leq C_{N,t}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}+\lambda}.

For the term
R_{2}
, we decompose in
O_{2}
the term

\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{\rho}^{t}\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:fact})}{=}\left(\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{K}^{t}\right)\otimes\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right)\right)\otimes\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{K_{0}}^{t}\right).

For the transverse part
\tilde{\Phi}=d\tilde{\phi}_{K}^{t}+d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}
,
Lemma
C.35
in the appendix gives that (and this is the
main relation that underlies Theorem
3.15
)

\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{K}^{t}\right)\otimes\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right)=\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(\tilde{\Phi}\right)\underset{(\ref{eq:expression_Phi_circ-1})}{=}\mathcal{P}_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}^{-1}\right)^{\circ}}\left(\Upsilon\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{K}^{t}\right)\right)^{1/2}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right)\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}\right)^{\circ}}\mathcal{P}_{\rho}.

If we add the neutral component
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{K_{0}}^{t}\right)

that has no effect this gives

\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{\rho}^{t}\right)=\mathcal{P}_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)}\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}^{-1}\right)^{\circ}}\Upsilon_{t}^{1/2}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}_{\rho}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right)\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}\right)^{\circ}}\mathcal{P}_{\rho}.

This shows that
R_{2}\underset{(\ref{eq:def_R2-1})}{=}0
.Finally for the term

R_{3}
, we add the cut-off
\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}
that has no effect
from our assumptions on points
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
, we pass from the Euclidean
metric to the metric
g
repeating arguments with the slow variation
of the metric (
2.59
) and get that
R_{3}\leq C_{N,t}\left\langle\mathrm{dist}_{g}\left(\rho^{\prime},\tilde{\phi}^{t}\left(\rho\right)\right)\right\rangle^{-N}\left\langle\left|\rho\right|\right\rangle^{-\frac{1}{2}+\lambda}

for any
0<\lambda<\frac{1}{2}
, as well.

From these estimates and assumption (
3.42
) on
\rho,\rho^{\prime}
,
we can add the cut-off
\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\Sigma,\sigma}\right)

and deduce that for any
0<\lambda<\frac{1}{2}
,

\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\Sigma,\sigma}\right)\left(e^{tX}-\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\Delta}\,\Upsilon_{t}^{1/2}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right)\mathcal{T}_{N}\right)\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\Sigma,\sigma}\right)\in\Psi_{\tilde{\phi}^{t}}^{-\frac{1}{2}+\lambda}.

From Definition
3.12
, this gives (
3.40
).
∎

3.5
Description of the operator
e^{tX}
near the set
\Sigma
with
the bundle
T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M

In this section we provide another expression for the operator
e^{tX}

similar to (
3.40
) but sometimes more convenient, see
Remark
3.20
below. The little difference with
(
3.40
) is that we will use the bundle
T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\underset{(\ref{eq:decomp_K_K0_N})}{=}T\Sigma\oplus N\rightarrow\Sigma

instead of the sub-bundle
N\rightarrow\Sigma
.

Similarly to (
3.34
), we introduce the following cut-off
function in frequency
\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}:T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\rightarrow\left[0,1\right]

defined as follows. For
v\in T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M
, and
\omega=\boldsymbol{\omega}\left(\pi\left(v\right)\right)
,

\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}\left(v\right)=\boldsymbol{1}_{\left\{\left\|v\right\|_{g}\leq\left\langle\omega\right\rangle^{\mu/2},\left|\omega\right|\geq\omega_{0}\right\}}.

(3.44)

The multiplication operator by
\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}
is also denoted

\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}:\mathcal{S}\left(T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\right)
.

We denote
r_{/\Sigma}:\mathcal{S}\left(TT^{*}M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\right)

the restriction operator to the base space
\Sigma\subset T^{*}M
.
We denote

r_{N}:\mathcal{S}\left(T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\left(N\right)

(3.45)

the restriction operator to the sub-bundle
N\subset T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M
.

Definition 3.18
.

We define the operators

\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}:=\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}r_{/\Sigma}\left(\widetilde{\exp^{\circ}}\right)\mathcal{T}\quad:C^{\infty}\left(M\right)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}^{\prime}\left(T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\right)

(3.46)

\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\Delta}:=\mathcal{T}^{\dagger}\widetilde{\left(\exp_{N}^{-1}\right)^{\circ}}r_{N}\chi_{\Sigma}^{\mu}\quad:\mathcal{S}\left(T_{\Sigma}T^{*}M\right)\rightarrow C^{\infty}\left(M\right).

(3.47)

Theorem 3.19
.

For any
t\in\mathbb{R}
, we have

e^{tX}\approx\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}^{\Delta}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(\left(d\tilde{\phi}^{t}\right)_{/\Sigma}\right)\mathcal{T}_{\Sigma}

(3.48)

Remark 3.20
.

This remark is very similar to the previous
Remark
2.18
. One advantage of (
3.48
)
compared to (
3.40
) is that it does not contain the time
dependent metaplectic correction
\Upsilon_{t}^{1/2}
, but contains
instead, through the decomposition (
3.29
), the operator

\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{T\Sigma}^{t}\right)
that
itself contains
\Upsilon_{t}^{1/2}
. Later in the proof of Theorem

4.31
, we will use and take advantage of some
unitary properties of this operator
\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{T\Sigma}^{t}\right)
.
On the other hand, one advantage of (
3.40
) compare to
(
3.48
) is that it deals directly with the normal
sub-bundle
N
on which we aim to work, so it is easier at first
sight.

Démonstration
.

The proof follows the same lines as the proof of Theorem
3.15
.
The only difference is that instead of using Lemma
C.35
,
we use Lemma
C.37
in the appendix for the
Euclidean and linear case.
∎

3.6
Emergence of quantum mechanics
near the set
\Sigma
for a general contact vector field

A direct consequence of Theorem
3.15
is
the following theorem that shows that at any time
t
the dynamics
of the pull back operator
e^{tX_{F}}
is well approximated microlocally
near the set
\Sigma
(at distance less than
\sigma
) and at high
frequencies
\omega
, by a quantum operator
\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\Delta}\,\Upsilon_{t}^{1/2}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right)\mathcal{T}_{N}

(this is a F.I.O., compare with (
1.11
)).

This result is less precise than Theorem
1.5

that gives an approximation of the full operator
e^{tX_{F}}
, whereas
here we have an approximation for a restriction to a micro-local neighborhood
of
\Sigma
. However this may be interesting because
\Sigma
is
an invariant set for the dynamics. We may wonder if with some additional
generic assumptions, this result can be improved towards Theorem
1.5
.

Theorem 3.21
.

\forall t\in\mathbb{R}
,
\exists m<0,

\forall\sigma>0
,
\exists C_{t,\sigma}>0

\forall\omega>0
,

\left\|\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\Sigma,\sigma}\right)\left(e^{tX_{F}}-\mathcal{T}_{N}^{\Delta}\,\Upsilon_{t}^{1/2}\tilde{\mathrm{Op}}\left(d\tilde{\phi}_{N}^{t}\right)\mathcal{T}_{N}\right)\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\Sigma,\sigma}\right)\left(\mathrm{Id}-\mathrm{Op}\left(\chi_{\omega}\right)\right)\right\|_{L^{2}\left(M;F\right)}\leq C_{t}\omega^{m}\underset{\omega\rightarrow+\infty}{\rightarrow}0.

Démonstration
.

From Theorem
3.15
, Definition
3.12

and Lemma
2.12
.
∎

4
Micro-local analysis of a
contact Anosov vector field
X
near
\Sigma=\mathbb{R}\mathcal{A}\backslash\left\{0\right\}

In this section, as a last step, we consider the model of interest
for this paper: a smooth contact Anosov vector field
X
on a closed
manifold
M
. In other words, we add the hypothesis of Anosov dynamics
to the previous section
3
. We first
recall the precise definitions and notations.

4.1
Definitions and notations

4.1.1
Anosov vector field

We make the hypothesis that
X
is a smooth
Anosov vector
field
on a closed manifold
M
. This means that for every point

m\in M
the tangent vector
X\left(m\right)
is non zero and we
have a continuous splitting of the tangent space

T_{m}M=E_{u}\left(m\right)\oplus E_{s}\left(m\right)\oplus\underbrace{E_{0}\left(m\right)}_{\mathbb{R}X\left(m\right)}

(4.1)

that is invariant under the action of the differential of the flow
map
d\phi^{t}:TM\rightarrow TM
and there exist
\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}>0
,

C>0
and a smooth metric
g_{M}
on
M
such that

\forall t\geq 0,\forall m\in M,\quad\left\|d\phi_{/E_{u}\left(m\right)}^{-t}\right\|_{g_{M}}\leq Ce^{-\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}t},\quad\left\|d\phi_{/E_{s}\left(m\right)}^{t}\right\|_{g_{M}}\leq Ce^{-\lambda_{\mathrm{min}}t}.

(4.2)

See Figure
4.1
. The linear subspace
E_{u}\left(m\right),E_{s}\left(m\right)\subset T_{m}M

are called the unstable/stable spaces and the one dimensional space

E_{0}\left(m\right):=\mathbb{R}X\left(m\right)
is called the neutral
